Research use only — not a diagnosis. This report combines automated lesion segmentation with literature associations from the aphasia knowledge base. It does not diagnose aphasia, predict an individual's outcome or prescribe therapy; any clinical use requires review by a qualified clinician.
  • Lesion mask: SynthStroke (MNI) SynthStroke_in_MNI.nii.gz. QC: not rated in the QC checkpoint.
  • How this lesion mask was made:
    1. Brain mask: HD-BET (hdbet/1.0.0), fast mode.
    2. Segmented by SynthStroke (synthstroke/0.0.0, synth-plus weights, with test-time augmentation) on the T1w sub-M2004_ses-524_acq-tfl3p2_run-3_T1w.nii.gz; predicted voxels outside the brain mask dilated by 2 voxels were removed.
    3. Warped to the FSL MNI152 template (MNI152NLin6Asym, 1 mm) with LINDA's registration of this T1w (native → LINDA's template → ch2 → MNI152, one nearest-neighbour resampling).
  • Template space: Lesion masks are on the FSL MNI152 template (MNI152NLin6Asym, 1 mm); the registration used for this mask is named above. Atlases are resampled to the lesion by world coordinates. Overlaps still depend on how well this individual brain was registered to MNI152, which matters most for small or thin regions.

This participant’s measures: what the evidence saysOpen sectionClose section

Findings about measures such as lesion volume or aphasia severity report associations in study groups (for example, larger lesions with more severe aphasia), not predictions for one person.

Brain-health interpretation boundary: A single scan without an age- and acquisition-matched reference cannot establish atrophy. Stroke cavities can mimic tissue loss; the lesion adjustment is not a reconstruction of pre-stroke anatomy. FLAIR hyperintensity outside the stroke-exclusion zone is a WMH candidate, not an automatic diagnosis of small-vessel disease, and no Fazekas grade is inferred.
Review flags: stroke lesion overlaps SynthSeg lateral-ventricle labels; raw ventricle volumes may reflect the cavity.

Author-reported limitations

  • Primary purpose was to characterise latent lesion-behaviour structure rather than optimise prediction accuracy.
  • Language impairment quantified using WAB-R only — a coarse measure; other batteries might reveal a different anatomical structure.
  • Neuroimaging modalities were not combined within a single model; additive multimodal benefit was not tested.
  • Findings do not represent an exhaustive overview of all contributing regions.

Extraction flags

  • cohort overlaps with @Fridriksson2018, @Yourganov2015Predicting (USC/MUSC Aphasia Lab, POLAR trial NCT03416738); flag for downstream double-counting risk.
  • left_perisylvian_network is a forward-looking target ID — does not yet exist as a canonical entry.

Author-reported limitations

Author-reported limitations

  • Lesion volume measured in MNI-normalised space (warping inflates apparent volume relative to native space).
  • Automated lesion identification (Seghier 2008) detects abnormal-tissue-class voxels — captures gliosis and atrophy alongside the primary lesion.
  • U-threshold of 0.5 was used (vs. default 0.3) — different from many other groups' pipelines.

page 217 · Methods 2.1 — Participants · supports sample

Each patient's lesion was automatically identified using a fully automated method based on fuzzy clustering (Seghier et al., 2008). The default parameters were used aside from the lesion definition 'U-threshold', which was set to 0.5 rather than 0.3 to create a binary lesion image.

page 220 · Methods 2.5 · supports method

by partialling out lesion volume there is a high risk for type II error. Hence, all VBCM analyses were performed and reported in this paper once with the behaviours of interest only and once with a correction for lesion volume.

Ci 95

P Value
p < .05

Author-reported limitations

  • Small convenience sample (n=18); retrospective design limits causal inference.
  • Sex violated normality assumption and was removed from the regression model.
  • Three participants imaged via CT rather than MRI, reducing lesion volume precision.
  • Psychosocial measures collected only at follow-up, not initial assessment, precluding temporal inference about these predictors.
  • Two participants initially assessed at the cusp of the chronic threshold (5 MPO); excluding them attenuated psychosocial predictor effects.

Extraction flags

  • 3 of 18 participants had CT not MRI — lesion volume measure heterogeneous

Review provenance

Entry status: approved · Review provenance: human · Reviewer: human:michele

Source passages

lesion volume was the only significant predictor of proportion change in AQ. The negative regression weight indicates that smaller lesions yield larger language gains in the long term, after controlling for other predictors included in the model.

page 5 · Methods – Participants · supports sample

Lesions obtained via MRI were manually segmented on the structural image by a trained technician, and checked for accuracy by an experienced neurologist blinded to the behavioral data. Lesion tracings were then warped to a standard atlas space (i.e., the Montreal Neurological Institute (MNI) Colin27 template) using a symmetric diffeomorphic registration algorithm.

Author-reported limitations

  • ALA collected only at follow-up — cannot determine whether participation satisfaction is stable or situational.
  • Counterintuitive finding: ALA aphasia domain (perceived impairment severity) negatively related to outcome — more perceived severity associated with better gains. May reflect baseline severity or personal attitudes.
  • Excluding the 2 participants at the cusp of chronic threshold attenuated psychosocial predictor significance.

Extraction flags

  • Exact coefficients pulled from Table 3 (b/SE/β); paper reports per-coefficient p-values via asterisk-coding (** = p<.05, *** = p<.01) rather than numeric p
  • Psychosocial measures assessed only at follow-up, not at initial assessment — temporal directionality of this predictor is uncertain
  • Combined model (lesion vol + ALA participation + ALA aphasia) F(4,13)=6.96, p=.003, R²adj=0.58 — strongest model in paper

Lesion size was measured in mm3 after warping to a standardized template and then entered as a voxelwise continuous predictor variable in a group analysis.

page 4 · fMRI Analysis · supports method

All whole-brain analyses were examined at cluster corrected P < 0.01

page 4 · fMRI Analysis · supports statistics

MRI data were collected on a 3.0 T Siemens Trio Scanner... Registration and normalization were carried out to the MNI standardized brain provided by FSL.

page 4 · Scanning Parameters and Preprocessing · supports imaging details

This effect may not reflect plasticity at all but rather the increased effort required for language tasks by people with larger lesions.

page 12 · General Discussion · supports limitation

Knowledge-base entries needed: predictor entries for brain_parenchymal_fraction, brain_tissue_volume_ml, intracranial_volume_ml, lateral_ventricle_fraction, left_lateral_ventricle_ml, lesion_adjusted_brain_parenchymal_fraction, lesion_cavity_ml, lesion_masked_lateral_ventricle_fraction, lesion_ventricle_overlap_ml, right_lateral_ventricle_ml would be required before CALMaR could connect these measures to curated evidence.

Lesion location: what the evidence saysOpen sectionClose section

This section connects the participant’s lesion location to published group-level findings about symptoms. Aphasia diagnoses, subtype labels and other non-symptom targets are excluded: this report does not infer the presence or type of aphasia from a lesion. Start with the symptom summaries, then open a damaged region to inspect each supporting, null or mixed finding.

A region counts as damaged when it holds ≥5% of the lesion or ≥25% of it is destroyed. Regions are matched to the knowledge base through the curated atlas mapping (stroke-aphasia-kb/atlas_mapping/). Of the knowledge base's 461 location findings, 45 concern regions this lesion damages before the symptom-only display filter; 36 symptom findings are shown. 358 concern regions it does not damage, and 10 concern regions no atlas can represent (e.g. the extreme capsule).

Lesion size first: this lesion is 36.7 mL and the matched findings concern 8 damaged knowledge-base regions. Larger lesions damage more regions and therefore match more findings, whatever their location, and lesion volume is itself one of the stronger correlates of severity. Read the number of findings below as partly a measure of lesion size.

What does the display signal mean?

Project-specific display heuristic, not an evidence grade. It is used only to order these summaries. The calculation combines the proportion of each matched region damaged with the stored finding strength, study evidence class, extraction confidence and relationship direction. Lesion-mapping findings whose study did not control (or did not state controlling) lesion volume, or used no stated multiple-comparison correction, count for less. Finding counts do not measure certainty, and findings may come from the same study or overlapping cohorts.

Display signal: a project-specific ordering heuristic, not an evidence grade. It combines lesion overlap with stored finding strength, study evidence class, extraction confidence and relationship direction. Counts do not measure certainty, and records may share studies or cohorts.

Primary display relevance criterion: a symptom appears in the main list when its matched findings span at least 2 independent evidence groups (citation keys, with papers from the same or overlapping cohorts counted once) and 2 distinct anatomy mappings after KB synonyms are merged. Association, inverse, null, mixed and unclassified findings all count toward this display threshold. This is a scanning rule, not a certainty threshold; cohort overlap is only as complete as the knowledge base records it.

Speech production phonological 1 finding

Associations 1

Lesions in the left supramarginal gyrus and adjacent supra-Sylvian cortex (extending anteriorly into inferior postcentral, precentral, and premotor cortex) are associated with phonological/articulatory speech production deficits in chronic post-stroke aphasia, as captured by a Speech Production factor loading on word repetition, nonword repetition, and phonological error rate in picture naming.

P Value
not_reported

Author-reported limitations

  • Did not find evidence for inferior prefrontal cortex (Broca's area) in the dorsal production route — differs from some influential dual-pathway models.
  • Cross-sectional design; cannot infer recovery trajectory.
  • Only left hemisphere studied.
  • Anatomical alignment of lesions does not guarantee functional alignment.
  • VLSM lesion overlap requires ≥10 participants; low-coverage regions may be undetectable.

Extraction flags

  • Exact MNI peak coordinates not reported numerically; approximate range read from Figure 4 panel labels.
  • t-statistic range read from Figure 4 colour bar (blue-green scale); verify in supplementary.
  • Replication citations (Schwartz 2012, Buchsbaum 2011, Fridriksson 2010) cited by Mirman et al. in Discussion — check exact citation keys in citations.md.
  • Note that Broca's area was specifically NOT found in this VLSM — important negative finding documented in region_definition.description.

Review provenance

Entry status: approved · Review provenance: legacy approval · Reviewer: claude-cowork

Source passages

VLSM of the Speech Production factor identiﬁed a region superior to the Sylvian ﬁssure, primarily in the supramarginal gyrus and extending anteriorly into inferior postcentral, pre- central and premotor cortex (Fig. 4, blue–green). These regions form part of the dorsal language pathway of dual-pathway models and have been shown in other reports to be involved in speech production.

page 4 · Results – Voxel-based lesion–symptom mapping · supports claim

In the present study, the dorsal speech production route extends superior to the Sylvian ﬁssure, from the supramarginal gyrus through inferior postcentral and precentral sensorimotor regions to premotor cortical regions involved in articulatory motor control.

page 5 · Discussion · supports claim

We did not ﬁnd evidence to support the inclusion of inferior prefrontal cortex (Broca's area), posterior planum temporale or posterior superior temporal gyrus (Wernicke's area) in the dorsal speech production route.

page 5 · Discussion · supports region definition

The peri-Sylvian regions involved in phonological processing were further subdivided into supra-Sylvian regions for speech production and infra-Sylvian regions for speech recognition.

page 5 · Discussion · supports claim

The sample consisted of 43 women and 56 men, 48 African Americans and 51 Caucasians. They averaged 58 years of age (s.d. ¼ 11; range ¼ 26–79), 14 years of education (s.d. ¼ 3; range ¼ 10–21) and 53 months post onset of stroke (s.d. ¼ 68; range ¼ 1–381).

page 7 · Methods – Participants · supports sample

VLSM analysis was performed by running a simple regression analysis at each voxel, with the lesion status as the independent variable and the factor score as the dependent variable. To control the effect of total lesion volume, each lesion map was normalized to have a unit norm.

page 7 · Methods – Lesion analysis · supports method

The derived statistical p-map was thresholded with FDR q ¼ 0.05 (Semantic Recognition factor was thresholded with FDR q ¼ 0.1) and isolated clusters with voxel number no more than 50 after thresholding were removed.

page 8 · Methods – Lesion analysis · supports statistics

Anatomical alignment of lesions does not guarantee functional alignment due to individual differences in functional organization and re-organization following stroke; however, large-scale studies such as this one provide the best evidence regarding aspects of functional organization that are consistent across individuals.

page 7 · Discussion – Limitations · supports limitation

VLSM analysis was performed by running a simple regression analysis at each voxel, with the lesion status as the independent variable and the factor score as the dependent variable.

page 7 · added by backfill · supports method

Only voxels lesioned in at least 10 participants were included in analysis

page 7 · added by backfill · supports method

We call this approach ‘direct total lesion volume control’.

page 8 · added by backfill · supports method

Phonological production 1 finding

Author-reported limitations

  • JHU atlas does not subdivide insula into subregions (SPGI vs. other); limits comparison with Dronkers (1996)
  • Continuous T1 signal analysis may introduce error if right-hemisphere diaschisis altered signal
  • Cohort includes patients with dysarthria (rated but excluded from main analysis)
  • Single rater for behavioral scoring (though ICC=0.884 established)

Extraction flags

  • USC/MUSC cohort; likely overlaps with @DenOuden2019 and @Fridriksson2018 (same lab group)
  • Continuous T1 signal intensity used rather than binary lesion masks — unusual method, reduces comparability
  • No separate JHU insula subregion; Dronkers SPGI cannot be directly tested

Author-reported limitations

  • Speech-language assessments qualitative; inter-rater reliability not formally examined
  • T2/FLAIR at acute stage may underestimate lesion extent compared to later imaging
  • AOS diagnosis complicated in patients with concomitant aphasia (phonemic paraphasia shares features with AOS)
  • Lesion functional effects may extend beyond visible infarction area (diaschisis)

Extraction flags

  • Japanese-speaking cohort; acute/subacute phase (median 7 days); no cohort overlap with USC papers
  • Qualitative AOS assessment only; no standardized rating scale (unlike Basilakos 2015)
  • Independent replication of Basilakos (2015) left precentral gyrus finding

individuals with unscorable naturalistic language samples were excluded... consequently means that our sample was skewed towards participants with less severe speech-language impairments

page 9 · Limitations · supports limitation

White-matter disconnection: normative lesion-based modelsOpen sectionClose section

This section shows white-matter tracts that may be interrupted beyond the visible lesion. Open a tract to inspect its anatomical context and any group-level findings linked to it.

The BCBToolkit map gives, for every voxel, the probability that its fibres pass through the lesion. A tract counts as disconnected when ≥50% of its voxels have a probability above 0.5. This catches tracts cut by the lesion away from where it lies, which direct overlap misses. Relationship counts summarise the stored group findings for each tract: association means a group-level study reported a relationship; inverse association means the stored relationship runs in the opposite direction; null means the study did not find the association; mixed means results were inconsistent; and other / unclassified means no display direction was assigned. A cross-reference means the same symptom target also has direct-damage evidence from a different anatomy in this participant’s lesion-location section. It is not independent or additive evidence and does not predict the participant’s symptoms.

Where this evidence comes from
  1. The participant-specific SynthStroke mask is the input.
  2. CALMaR reads its BCBToolkit probability map (Disconnectome_synthstroke.nii.gz).
  3. That map is overlapped with the JHU and XTRACT tract atlases using the threshold above.
  4. Literature claims come from the tract/region entries in stroke-aphasia-kb/, linked through the curated stroke-aphasia-kb/atlas_mapping/; each finding retains its citation and review details.

The participant map calculation and the literature claims are separate provenance layers. Group associations do not predict this participant’s outcome.

This participant is in the chronic phase. Of these 47 findings, 28 were studied in the same phase, 16 in another phase (marked “stage mismatch”) and 3 do not state it.

Left Arcuate Fasciculus and Superior Longitudinal Fascicle (Dorsal Language Pathway) (left)99% of the tract disconnected · also directly damaged (63% destroyed) · 8 distinct findingsAssociations 8Same symptom targets also listed under direct damage elsewhere: Fluency (speech fluency), Phonological production, Syntactic Processing Deficits in AphasiaShow findingsHide findings

Same symptom targets elsewhere in the direct-damage evidence

  • Fluency (speech fluency) (this tract: 2 associations; direct damage elsewhere: 3 associations)
  • Phonological production (this tract: 2 associations; direct damage elsewhere: 3 associations)
  • Syntactic Processing Deficits in Aphasia (this tract: 1 association; direct damage elsewhere: 1 association)

These links cross-reference target labels; they do not make the two evidence paths independent or additive.

Entry status: approved · Review provenance: legacy approval · Reviewer: claude-cowork

Full citation for @Marchina2011

Marchina S, Zhu LL, Norton A, Zipse L, Wan CY, Schlaug G. Impairment of speech production predicted by lesion load of the left arcuate fasciculus. Stroke. 2011;42(8):2251–2256. doi:10.1161/STROKEAHA.110.606103

Study context, limitations, review and source passages

Sample

N
30
Population
chronic stroke survivors with left-hemisphere MCA infarct and residual speech production impairments
Time Post Onset
>=11 months post-stroke; mean 35.0 months (SD 28.7)
Age Range
mean age 58.5 years (SD 10.0)
Handedness
all right-handed
Language
not_reported (Boston, MA recruitment — English assumed)
Recruitment
patients enrolled in Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center / Harvard Medical School Stroke Recovery and Neuroimaging Laboratory
Inclusion Criteria
right-handed; left-hemispheric stroke in MCA territory; >=11 months post-stroke; diagnosis of severe nonfluent aphasia in acute/subacute phase; auditory comprehension >=45th percentile on BDAE combined Auditory Comprehension subtests; Raven's Colored Progressive Matrices >=50th percentile.
Exclusion Criteria
bihemispheric or brain stem infarcts; primary intracerebral hemorrhages; previous or subsequent strokes; concomitant neurological diseases/disorders; pure anomia; aphasia syndromes with severe comprehension deficits (<45th percentile BDAE); cognitive impairment (<50th percentile Raven's CPM).
Phase
chronic
Cohort
not_reported

Reported statistics

Threshold
multiple regression with all 4 predictors (lesion size, AF lesion load, EmC lesion load, UF lesion load); p<0.05 for partial R² of individual predictor.
Effect Size
partial R²=0.175, p=0.030 (AF lesion load predicting WPM); overall model adjusted R²=0.301, p=0.011
Ci 95
not_reported
P Value
0.030

Author-reported limitations

  • Lesion load method uses probabilistic tracts from only 10 healthy controls; individual variability in tract anatomy not fully captured.
  • Cross-sectional design: cannot establish whether AF lesion load predicts recovery trajectory or only current impairment level.
  • All patients had left-hemisphere MCA strokes and chronic aphasia — generalizability to acute stroke, subcortical hemorrhage, or right-hemisphere damage is limited.
  • Behavioral measures (WPM, %CIUs, CIUs/min) are not purely dissociable along the dorsal/ventral stream lines predicted by dual-stream models; all correlated with AF load but not EmC/UF load.
  • Study does not establish causation between AF damage and speech impairment in the strict experimental sense — it is a correlational lesion-load analysis.

Extraction flags

  • Relationship coded as `correlational` per KB rules: DTI tractography + lesion load analysis tracks structural integrity against behavior but is not a strict causal VLSM/LSM approach — the probabilistic tracts come from healthy controls, not directly from the patients' own DTI.
  • Talairach space used in paper (not MNI152); ROI coordinates reported are x-values only (sagittal slice positions).
  • 10 healthy controls contributed DTI data for probabilistic maps; patient n=30 contributed behavioral and T1 lesion data only.

Entry status: approved · Review provenance: legacy approval · Reviewer: claude-cowork

Source passages

Regression analyses showed that arcuate fasciculus lesion load, but not extreme capsule or uncinate fasciculus lesion load or overall lesion size, significantly predicted rate, informativeness, and overall efficiency of speech as well as naming ability.

page 1 · Abstract · supports claim

A regression analysis was first conducted using lesion size and lesion loads of all 3 tracts (ie, AF, EmC, and UF) as predictors of words/min (adjusted R²=0.301, P=0.011). AF lesion load proved to be the best variable (partial R²=0.175, P=0.030; Figure 2A), whereas EmC lesion load (partial R²=0.087, P=0.135), UF lesion load (partial R²=0.098, P=0.112), and lesion size (partial R²=0.002, P=0.829) were shown to be nonsignificant predictors.

page 3 · Results — Rate of Speech · supports statistics

The study group consisted of 30 right-handed patients, all of whom had left-hemispheric strokes in the middle cerebral artery territory and were at least 11 months post stroke at the time of testing (6 females and 24 males; mean age 58.5 years [SD 10.0]; mean time poststroke 35.0 months [SD 28.7]).

page 2 · Methods — Subjects · supports sample

The control subjects underwent diffusion tensor imaging using a single-shot, spin-echo echoplanar imaging sequence with the following parameters: TR=10 seconds; TE=86.9 ms; resolution 2.6×2.6×2.6 mm³; 30 noncollinear diffusion directions with a b-value of 1000 s/mm²; and 6 acquisitions with a value of 0 s/mm².

page 2 · Methods — MRI and Diffusion Tensor Imaging Acquisition · supports imaging details

For the AF, a curved fiber bundle that connects the posterior portion of the temporoparietal junction with the frontal cortex, we drew 1 region of interest on the Fractional Anisotropy (FA) map in the white matter underlying the posterior middle and superior temporal gyri at approximately x=−50 mm (MNI space); a second region of interest was drawn on the same sagittal slice in the white matter underlying the pars opercularis of the posterior inferior frontal gyrus.

page 2 · Methods — MRI and Diffusion Tensor Imaging Acquisition · supports region definition

The binary fiber tracts of the 10 healthy control subjects were summed to generate a fiber map using Matlab (Figure 1). Voxel intensities ranged from I=0 (ie, voxel is not part of the tract in any of the subjects) to I=10 (ie, voxel is part of the tract in all 10 subjects); thus, the probability that a particular voxel would be part of the tract was calculated as one tenth of the voxel's intensity.

page 3 · Methods — Lesion Load Calculation · supports method

Author-reported limitations

  • Behavioral measures (WPM, %CIUs, CIUs/min) are not purely dissociable along dorsal/ventral stream lines; the authors acknowledge this may explain why all three correlated with AF rather than UF/EmC.
  • Small sample (n=30); regression with 4 predictors risks overfitting.
  • Cross-sectional design precludes recovery trajectory predictions.

Extraction flags

  • Relationship coded as `correlational` per KB rules: DTI tractography + lesion load analysis.
  • Strongest statistical result of the paper (partial R²=0.336, p=0.002) — informativeness had the largest AF lesion-load effect size.

Ci 95
not_reported
P Value
0.039

Author-reported limitations

  • The finding that AF (a dorsal-stream tract) predicts naming, typically considered a ventral-stream function, is discussed by the authors as possibly reflecting that their behavioral measures do not purely capture one stream.
  • UF nonsignificant trend (p=0.073) is consistent with prior work suggesting UF involvement in naming, but the study is underpowered to confirm this.
  • BNT administered as untimed; partial credit scoring used (0.5 for cued response, 0.25 for written-word identification) — this modified scoring may limit comparability.

Extraction flags

  • Relationship coded as `correlational` per KB rules.
  • Note: paper reports R² (not partial R²) for AF predicting BNT, while earlier outcomes used partial R². The paper text says 'AF lesion load (R²=0.159)' — coded as stated; may reflect total vs partial R² inconsistency in reporting.
  • UF trend (p=0.073) not significant but noted — consistent with dual-stream predictions for naming; not coded as a separate finding given it did not reach threshold.
  • retargeted confrontation_naming → anomia (symptom, v2.8) on 2026-10-26

Author-reported limitations

page 8 · Discussion · supports limitation

Participant diffusion tract-microstructure measurementsOpen sectionClose section

These are measurements from this participant’s own directional diffusion acquisition. They complement, but do not validate or numerically combine with, the BCB/DeepDisco disconnectome predictions.

FA and MD summarize diffusion within the successfully mapped portion of each tract; lesion overlap is reported separately when it can be mapped onto the same grid. In stroke, oedema, cavitation, crossing fibres, distortion and tracking failure can all change these values or prevent reconstruction. A low streamline count or missing tract must therefore not be read as proof of anatomical disconnection. Acquisition: 63 diffusion-weighted volumes · shells 0/1000 · QSIPrep 26.1.1 + QSIRecon 26.0.1 · tortoise motion/eddy correction · diffusion session ses-780 · lesion/anatomy session ses-524.

Review flags: diffusion QC status: requires visual review; values are provisional; no opposite phase-encoding DWI; susceptibility correction is limited; diffusion and anatomical lesion mask come from different sessions; lesion-to-bundle overlap and valid-voxel coverage are not yet calculated; patient-specific bundle absence is not evidence of anatomical disconnection.

How to read the evidenceOpen sectionClose section

Display signal is CALMaR’s project-specific ordering heuristic, not a formal evidence grade or participant-level prediction. Its basis is explained beside the outcome summaries.

Relationship is what a study reported between the region or measure and the outcome: association, inverse association, no association or mixed results. Evidence strength is the extractor’s reading of the finding. Extraction confidence is confidence that the paper was transcribed and interpreted correctly. These are distinct.

Finding counts are descriptive. They do not measure certainty, and findings do not necessarily represent independent studies or independent cohorts. Group associations do not predict this participant’s outcome.

An “atlas match includes a documented judgement call” note links a knowledge-base region to atlas units using a curated decision recorded in stroke-aphasia-kb/atlas_mapping/README.md.

WAB/WAB-R profile conversion: where a recorded type informed the domain table, + relatively preserved kept severity unassigned and − impaired became a “mild profile floor”. Neither is a measured domain severity.
Stage badge: Acute <7 d · Subacute 7 d–6 mo · Chronic >6 mo. A therapy-stage warning identifies evidence from a different recovery stage; open it for detail. Rules and their sources: stroke-aphasia-kb/scripts/aphasia_rules.py.
